### Inside-Out: Hidden Factual Knowledge in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15299v1)
- **Authors**: Zorik Gekhman, Eyal Ben David, Hadas Orgad, Eran Ofek, Yonatan Belinkov, Idan Szpector, Jonathan Herzig, Roi Reichart
- **Abstract**: This work presents a framework for assessing whether large language models (LLMs) encode more factual knowledge in their parameters than what they express in their outputs. While a few studies hint at this possibility, none has clearly defined or demonstrated this phenomenon. We first propose a formal definition of knowledge, quantifying it for a given question as the fraction of correct-incorrect answer pairs where the correct one is ranked higher. This gives rise to external and internal knowledge, depending on the information used to score individual answer candidates: either the model's observable token-level probabilities or its intermediate computations. Hidden knowledge arises when internal knowledge exceeds external knowledge. We then present a case study, applying this framework to three popular open-weights LLMs in a closed-book QA setup. Our results indicate that: (1) LLMs consistently encode more factual knowledge internally than what they express externally, with an average gap of 40%. (2) Surprisingly, some knowledge is so deeply hidden that a model can internally know an answer perfectly, yet fail to generate it even once, despite large-scale repeated sampling of 1,000 answers. This reveals fundamental limitations in the generation capabilities of LLMs, which (3) puts a practical constraint on scaling test-time compute via repeated answer sampling in closed-book QA: significant performance improvements remain inaccessible because some answers are practically never sampled, yet if they were, we would be guaranteed to rank them first.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Inside-Out: Hidden Factual Knowledge in LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a framework for assessing whether Large Language Models (LLMs) possess more factual knowledge in their parameters than they express in their outputs, termed "hidden knowledge."  The framework quantifies knowledge as the ability to rank correct answers above incorrect ones using a scoring method.  It distinguishes between external knowledge (based on observable token-level probabilities) and internal knowledge (based on intermediate computations). Hidden knowledge is defined as the existence of an internal function that ranks answers more accurately than any external function.  The authors conduct a case study with open-weight LLMs in a closed-book QA setup, finding evidence of hidden knowledge, limitations in generation capabilities, and constraints on performance improvements via test-time compute scaling. Specifically, they show that internal scoring methods outperform external ones, and that some correct answers, though perfectly known internally, are rarely generated.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of "hidden knowledge" isn't entirely new, as hinted at in prior works cited by the authors. However, the paper's primary contribution lies in its **formal definition and systematic framework** for quantifying and measuring this phenomenon.  This is a significant step forward, providing a concrete methodology where prior studies only alluded to the possibility. The novel approach of contrasting internal and external scoring functions to rigorously establish this gap is commendable.

*   **Significance:**  The findings have important implications for understanding LLMs and improving their performance:

    *   **Performance Improvement:** Demonstrating that LLMs encode more knowledge than they express suggests potential for developing techniques to "surface" this hidden knowledge, leading to improved accuracy and reliability. The paper's exploration of test-time computation through sampling and internal scoring, while showing some improvements, also reveals fundamental limitations due to the generation process.

    *   **Interpretability:**  The existence of hidden knowledge highlights the need to understand how knowledge is accessed (or suppressed) during inference, contributing to ongoing efforts to improve LLM interpretability.

    *   **Safety:** The paper’s suggestion that sensitive, unexpressed knowledge resides in LLM parameters highlights potential risks related to model safety.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous Framework:** The paper presents a well-defined and mathematically sound framework for analyzing hidden knowledge.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The case study on multiple open-weight LLMs provides strong evidence supporting the existence of hidden knowledge.
    *   **Practical Implications:**  The paper explores the potential for leveraging hidden knowledge to improve QA performance.
    *   **Analysis of Generative Limitations**: The identification of the model's inability to even sample answers it has fully encoded knowledge of highlights a fundamental limitation in generative techniques.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** The framework is computationally intensive, limiting the scale of the experiments (model size and data set size). This is acknowledged by the authors.
    *   **Definition Scope:** The focus on (subject, relation, object) triplets, while simplifying analysis, limits the generalizability of the findings to other forms of knowledge. The paper does make an effort to address this in the appendix.
    *   **Heuristic Probe Selection:** The internal scoring function is based on a probing classifier, which, though knowledge-aware, involves some heuristic choices in its training. There's a reliance on the assumption that greedy decoding implies model knowledge.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's framework and findings are likely to stimulate further research in several areas, including:

    *   Developing new methods to surface hidden knowledge in LLMs.
    *   Investigating the reasons for the variance in hidden knowledge across different LLM architectures and training methodologies.
    *   Exploring the relationship between hidden knowledge and model safety.
    *   Developing more effective decoding strategies to overcome the identified limitations in LLM generation capabilities.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **8/10**. The paper provides a solid foundation for analyzing and quantifying hidden knowledge in LLMs. While the concept itself was hinted at previously, the formalization, systematic framework, and empirical validation presented in this paper represent a significant advancement. The paper has some weaknesses like the computational cost, but the results have clear potential to influence future research on LLMs.

### aiXcoder-7B-v2: Training LLMs to Fully Utilize the Long Context in Repository-level Code Completion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15301v1)
- **Authors**: Jia Li, Hao Zhu, Huanyu Liu, Xianjie Shi, He Zong, Yihong Dong, Kechi Zhang, Siyuan Jiang, Zhi Jin, Ge Li
- **Abstract**: Repository-level code completion aims to complete code based on the long contexts of the repository. Existing studies extract long contexts from the repository as inputs and leverage Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate code. However, we reveal a severe limitation of LLMs, i.e., LLMs may ignore the information within long contexts in code completion. In other words, even the contexts contain useful information (e.g., relevant APIs or similar code), LLMs may fail to utilize this information. We think this limitation is caused by an inherent bias in LLMs, i.e., relying on nearby contexts and ignoring long-range contexts. To address this, we propose a novel fine-tuning approach named CoLT. The core idea of CoLT is to provide explicit supervision signals, which emphasize that long-range contexts may hold relevant information. Specifically, CoLT proposes a reinforcement learning-based training, which explicitly encourages models to utilize the information within long contexts and punishes models for ignoring long contexts. To support CoLT, we release CoLT-132K, a large-scale dataset with 132k samples across four languages, each containing long-context inputs. We apply CoLT to a popular LLM - aiXcoder-7B and release aiXcoder-7B-v2. We conduct extensive experiments on CoLT-132K and a public benchmark - CrossCodeEval. Our experiments yield the results: 1. Effectiveness. CoLT substantially improves aiXcoder-7B. aiXcoder-7B-v2 outperforms aiXcoder-7B by up to 44% in exact match. aiXcoder-7B-v2 becomes the state-of-the-art 7B model in code completion and even surpasses larger models. 2. Generalizability. The capability learned by CoLT can generalize to new languages. Besides, CoLT is model-agnostic and effectively improves multiple LLMs. 3. Enhanced Context Utilization Capability. CoLT significantly improves the capability of LLMs in utilizing the relevant information within long contexts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "aiXcoder-7B-v2: Training LLMs to Fully Utilize the Long Context in Repository-level Code Completion":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the issue that Large Language Models (LLMs) often fail to effectively utilize information within long contexts in repository-level code completion. The authors hypothesize that LLMs have an inherent bias towards nearby contexts, ignoring potentially useful information in long-range contexts.  They introduce a novel fine-tuning approach called COLT (Code Long-context Training) to mitigate this.  COLT uses reinforcement learning to explicitly encourage LLMs to utilize information in long contexts and penalize ignoring them.  To facilitate training, the authors create and release a large-scale dataset, COLT-132K, consisting of 132,000 repository-level code completion samples.  They apply COLT to aiXcoder-7B, creating aiXcoder-7B-v2, and demonstrate significant performance improvements over the original model and even surpassing larger models in some benchmarks.  They also demonstrate that the learned context utilization capabilities can generalize to new languages and other LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its identification of the long-context utilization problem in code completion and the development of the COLT fine-tuning approach. While reinforcement learning for code generation isn't entirely new, the specific application of DPO with a reward function designed to encourage long-range context utilization is a worthwhile contribution.  The creation and release of the COLT-132K dataset also adds value, providing a resource for training and evaluating long-context code completion models.
*   **Significance:** The paper's findings are significant because they highlight a critical limitation of applying LLMs directly to repository-level code completion. Addressing this limitation has practical implications for improving the accuracy and usefulness of code completion tools. The fact that a relatively small model (7B) can outperform much larger models after COLT fine-tuning is a valuable insight for resource-constrained settings. This is particularly interesting since many recent papers have been focussed on scaling LLMs.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Identification:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of LLMs struggling to utilize long-range context in code completion.
    *   **COLT Approach:** The COLT approach is well-motivated and seems to work. The use of reinforcement learning with an explicit reward that encourages long-context is a clever way to tackle the inherent bias of LLMs.
    *   **Extensive Experiments:** The paper provides extensive experimental results, including comparisons against a variety of baseline models and across different languages and scenarios.
    *   **Dataset Contribution:** The release of the COLT-132K dataset provides a valuable resource for the research community.
    *   **Model Agnostic:** The experimental results show that COLT generalizes to other models beyond just aixCoder-7B.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **The "Ground Truth" Problem:** Constructing a code completion dataset, where several answers could be correct and human preference plays a role, presents inherent difficulties. Although discussed, this remains a weakness.
    *   **Limited Ablation:** Although the method and experiments are thorough, there is no investigation into *why* exactly certain hyperparameters are important for the reinforcement learning. Are the learned APIs and their similarities reflected in a particular way within the attention layers of the transformer? The study stops at establishing a performance improvement, but doesn't deeply investigate *how* or *why*.
    *   **Reliance on Pre-trained Model:** The technique hinges on the capabilities of the base LLM, and its efficacy may be limited if the underlying LLM lacks the necessary pre-training or architecture to leverage long contexts.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence future research in code completion by shifting the focus from simply increasing model size or improving context retrieval to more effectively utilizing existing context. The release of the COLT-132K dataset will also likely stimulate further research in this area. The lessons learned in section 7 offer helpful practical information for applying this model in the real world.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of 8/10. The paper presents a novel approach to a significant problem in code completion, is well-executed with comprehensive experiments, and releases a valuable dataset. Its clear articulation of the long-context utilization problem and the effective solution in the form of COLT, coupled with its demonstration of improved performance and generalizability, warrants this high rating. The somewhat limited ablation analysis and reliance on a pre-trained LLM prevent it from scoring higher.

Smith, K. Tanidis, G. Testera, R. Teyssier, S. Tosi, A. Troja, M. Tucci, C. Valieri, D. Vergani, G. Verza, N. A. Walton
- **Abstract**: Light emission from galaxies exhibit diverse brightness profiles, influenced by factors such as galaxy type, structural features and interactions with other galaxies. Elliptical galaxies feature more uniform light distributions, while spiral and irregular galaxies have complex, varied light profiles due to their structural heterogeneity and star-forming activity. In addition, galaxies with an active galactic nucleus (AGN) feature intense, concentrated emission from gas accretion around supermassive black holes, superimposed on regular galactic light, while quasi-stellar objects (QSO) are the extreme case of the AGN emission dominating the galaxy. The challenge of identifying AGN and QSO has been discussed many times in the literature, often requiring multi-wavelength observations. This paper introduces a novel approach to identify AGN and QSO from a single image. Diffusion models have been recently developed in the machine-learning literature to generate realistic-looking images of everyday objects. Utilising the spatial resolving power of the Euclid VIS images, we created a diffusion model trained on one million sources, without using any source pre-selection or labels. The model learns to reconstruct light distributions of normal galaxies, since the population is dominated by them. We condition the prediction of the central light distribution by masking the central few pixels of each source and reconstruct the light according to the diffusion model. We further use this prediction to identify sources that deviate from this profile by examining the reconstruction error of the few central pixels regenerated in each source's core. Our approach, solely using VIS imaging, features high completeness compared to traditional methods of AGN and QSO selection, including optical, near-infrared, mid-infrared, and X-rays. [abridged]
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a novel approach for identifying Active Galactic Nuclei (AGN) and Quasars (QSOs) in astronomical images, specifically using diffusion-based inpainting on Euclid VIS data.  The core idea is to train a diffusion model on a large sample of galaxy images *without* labels. The model learns the underlying light distribution of typical galaxies.  By masking the central pixels of galaxy images and using the trained diffusion model to "inpaint" or reconstruct the central region, the authors identify sources whose central light distribution deviates significantly from the predicted profile. These deviations are considered potential AGN candidates. The paper explores different loss functions, mask sizes, and comparisons with existing methods to demonstrate the effectiveness of this technique. It also highlights the benefits of using this technique for obtaining high recall with an improved precision in identifying the fainter AGN.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The approach is relatively novel in the astronomical context. Diffusion models have become popular in machine learning, but their specific application to AGN identification from *single-band* VIS images is a significant innovation. While the paper acknowledges previous work using diffusion models for tasks like galaxy deconvolution, the inpainting approach to identify profile deviations is original in this particular astronomical application.
*   **Significance:**  If successful, this method has the potential to make an enormous impact on AGN research and particularly for future works with Euclid data. Euclid aims for wide and deep coverage, the volume of data is enormous and single band AGN candidate selection becomes extremely valuable because it is efficient. A method that can accurately and efficiently select AGN candidates from Euclid's VIS images could unlock an unprecedented sample of AGN for further study. The technique relies on a single optical band, it simplifies the identification process significantly compared to multi-wavelength methods and is computationally efficient, leveraging a pre-trained diffusion model for inference. This is valuable for surveys that may lack comprehensive multi-wavelength coverage.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   *Data Driven Approach:* The method is unsupervised in the sense that it does not explicitly require labelled AGN data for training. This allows the model to learn the general distribution of galaxies and flag outliers, minimizing any bias introduced from a limited training set.
    *   *High Recall:* The reported results show strong recall values, meaning the method is good at identifying a large fraction of known AGN.
    *   *Robustness:* Experiments with different loss functions and mask sizes highlight the robustness of the approach and provide insights into its behavior.
    *   *Euclid Relevance:*  Specifically designed for the Euclid VIS band, which offers high spatial resolution and depth over a large area, making the method directly applicable to this important dataset.
    *   *Computational Efficiency:* Single-band image processing lowers the computational cost significantly, which is great for applications to wide surveys such as Euclid.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   *Dependence on Galaxy Resolution:* The success of the method relies on well-resolved galaxies. At very high redshifts where galaxies are point-like, this approach would likely fail. The paper needs to address this limitation.
    *   *Reliance on Central Concentration:*  The core assumption is that AGN emission is centrally concentrated. While this is generally true, there are exceptions (e.g., obscured AGN, off-center AGN, dual AGN). The method's performance on such sources is unknown.
    *   *MSE Sensitivity:* The dependence on mean squared error (MSE) as a metric might be overly sensitive to specific pixels and less robust than other possible metrics.
    *   *Qualitative Validation:* It relies on known sources (i.e. galaxies, quasars) to check its results, but doesn't test the ability to discover new AGN systems that aren't in existing catalogues.

*   **Potential Improvements and Questions:**

    *   The paper needs to investigate the performance dependence on galaxy redshift more systematically.
    *   Exploring alternative metrics like structural similarity index measure (SSIM) in combination with MSE could be beneficial to improve the method's robustness.
    *   Adding a small amount of labeled data to fine-tune the diffusion model and classification boundary can improve its performance.
    *   It would be interesting to analyze why certain AGN are *not* identified by the method. Are these systematically different in any way?
    *   What are the biases in this method when it comes to AGN luminosity? Are they better at selecting very faint or extremely luminous ones?
    *   The trade off between the model with normalized loss and the one without a need to be explored even further. It may be worth exploring other variants on pixel values as a result of the process.

*   **Influence on the field:** The research has potential to significantly influence the way AGN are selected from large imaging surveys, paving the way for new studies of AGN evolution and their relation to galaxy formation.

**Score: 7.5/10**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and promising method for AGN identification that has significant potential for astronomical applications, especially within the context of the Euclid mission. The use of diffusion-based inpainting and the unsupervised approach to select AGN candidates are key strengths. The approach may offer improvements to the speed in cataloguing AGNs, which makes it a high-value candidate to be explored with Euclid. However, the limitations regarding the requirement of resolved galaxies, sensitivity to central concentration, and reliance on MSE metrics need to be carefully addressed.  The paper demonstrates significant technical skill and insight, but the areas identified for improvement suggest that there is still room for substantial advancement and optimization.

- **Classification**: astro-ph.GA
- **Score**: 7/10

### Solla: Towards a Speech-Oriented LLM That Hears Acoustic Context
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15338v1)
- **Authors**: Junyi Ao, Dekun Chen, Xiaohai Tian, Wenjie Feng, Jun Zhang, Lu Lu, Yuxuan Wang, Haizhou Li, Zhizheng Wu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently shown remarkable ability to process not only text but also multimodal inputs such as speech and audio. However, most existing models primarily focus on analyzing input signals using text instructions, overlooking scenarios in which speech instructions and audio are mixed and serve as inputs to the model. To address these challenges, we introduce Solla, a novel framework designed to understand speech-based questions and hear the acoustic context concurrently. Solla incorporates an audio tagging module to effectively identify and represent audio events, as well as an ASR-assisted prediction method to improve comprehension of spoken content. To rigorously evaluate Solla and other publicly available models, we propose a new benchmark dataset called SA-Eval, which includes three tasks: audio event classification, audio captioning, and audio question answering. SA-Eval has diverse speech instruction with various speaking styles, encompassing two difficulty levels, easy and hard, to capture the range of real-world acoustic conditions. Experimental results show that Solla performs on par with or outperforms baseline models on both the easy and hard test sets, underscoring its effectiveness in jointly understanding speech and audio.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SOLLA: Towards a Speech-Oriented LLM That Hears Acoustic Context":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SOLLA, a novel framework designed to improve Large Language Models (LLMs) ability to understand spoken instructions in conjunction with acoustic context (e.g., background audio events). SOLLA integrates an audio tagging module for identifying and representing audio events and an ASR-assisted prediction method to enhance comprehension of spoken content.  To evaluate SOLLA, the authors create SA-Eval, a new benchmark dataset with tasks like audio event classification, audio captioning, and audio question answering, featuring diverse speaking styles and difficulty levels (easy and hard) to reflect real-world acoustic conditions. The results demonstrate SOLLA's performance is comparable to or exceeds baseline models on both easy and hard test sets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of integrating audio event tagging directly into the training and inference process of an LLM is a sensible and practical advancement. Existing LLMs focused primarily on text instructions and limited acoustic understanding. SOLLA addresses a more realistic scenario where speech instructions exist alongside background audio, which the authors correctly identify as an underexplored area. The ASR-assisted prediction strategy is a reasonable enhancement. The specific architecture itself is not exceptionally groundbreaking, reusing established components like Whisper and LoRA, but the *integration* and task framing are the key contributions.

*   **Significance:** The significance is tied to the development of more practical and user-friendly speech-based interfaces. LLMs that can truly understand acoustic context are essential for seamless human-computer interaction in noisy and complex environments. SA-Eval is a valuable contribution, providing a standardized way to assess models in these scenarios. The dataset includes tasks, speech instructions, and different speaking styles. The study's rigor in creating easy/hard difficulty levels based on SNR strengthens the analysis.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem statement and motivation.
    *   Well-defined architecture with understandable components.
    *   The creation of SA-Eval benchmark is a strong contribution, addressing a gap in evaluation datasets.
    *   The experimental setup is clearly described and the baseline models are well-chosen.
    *   Ablation studies effectively demonstrate the contribution of individual components (AT module and ASR-assisted prediction).
    *   The paper is well-written and easy to understand.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited architectural novelty. The framework relies on existing components (Whisper, LoRA, InternLM2) rather than introducing truly novel building blocks. The contribution is mainly in how these components are connected and trained for the specific task.
    *   While the paper shows comparable or superior performance to baselines *on SA-Eval*, it's unclear how well SOLLA generalizes to other datasets or real-world applications.
    * The approach focuses exclusively on audio *events* as the relevant acoustic context. Real-world scenarios involve other crucial auditory cues like speaker diarization, emotion recognition from speech, and more complex soundscapes (music, overlapping speech, etc.). SOLLA's scope is relatively narrow in that respect.
    *  The authors acknowledge that their current SA-Eval benchmark only focuses on single-turn dialogs.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence future research by:

    *   Encouraging more research on LLMs that combine spoken instructions and acoustic context.
    *   Providing the SA-Eval dataset as a common benchmark for evaluating such models.
    *   Demonstrating the effectiveness of audio tagging modules and ASR-assisted prediction for this task.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** SOLLA introduces a pragmatic approach to a relevant problem: making LLMs more adept at understanding human speech in realistic environments. The SA-Eval dataset fills a critical gap for evaluating models in these settings. However, the limited architectural novelty (relying heavily on existing components) and narrow focus (primarily on audio events and only single-turn dialogues) prevent a higher score. The contributions are more incremental than revolutionary. While the paper provides a strong foundation, future work needs to expand the scope of acoustic understanding and develop more innovative model architectures.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: eess.AS
- **Score**: 7/10

### Uncertainty-Guided Chain-of-Thought for Code Generation with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15341v1)
- **Authors**: Yuqi Zhu, Ge Li, Xue Jiang, Jia Li, Hong Mei, Zhi Jin, Yihong Dong
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning has been demonstrated as an effective technique for improving the problem-solving capabilities of large language models (LLMs) in the context of code generation. However, existing CoT methods often exhibit a tendency toward "overthinking", where the LLM consistently applies reasoning strategies without adequately considering the task's underlying complexity. This results in the LLMs allocating excessive computational resources, in terms of tokens, to relatively simple tasks or problems where the correct answer is already evident. Additionally, this overthinking may lead LLMs down incorrect reasoning paths, resulting in incorrect code generation. In this paper, we introduce UnCertainty-Aware Chain-of-Thought (UnCert-CoT), an LLM-based approach designed to enhance code generation by incorporating an uncertainty-aware CoT reasoning mechanism, which focuses computational resources on targeting points where LLMs are more prone to error. We propose two confidence-based uncertainty measures: Entropy-based and Probability Differential-based methods. When uncertainty is high, UnCert-CoT activates CoT-decoding to generate multiple reasoning paths and selects the final code that exhibits the highest likelihood of correctness. In contrast, LLM directly generates the code when uncertainty is low. This uncertainty judgment mechanism allows LLMs to prioritize complex tasks and avoid unnecessary steps in simpler cases, thereby improving overall efficiency and accuracy in code generation. Our experimental results demonstrate that UnCert-CoT significantly enhances code generation accuracy on challenging benchmark MHPP(Mostly Hard Python Problems), it achieves improvements up to 6.1% on PassRate accuracy, particularly in situations where traditional LLMs are prone to errors.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Uncertainty-Guided Chain-of-Thought for Code Generation with LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UnCertainty-Aware Chain-of-Thought (UnCert-CoT), a novel approach to enhance code generation in Large Language Models (LLMs). UnCert-CoT aims to address the "overthinking" problem in existing Chain-of-Thought (CoT) methods, where LLMs unnecessarily apply reasoning steps to simple tasks, wasting computational resources and potentially introducing errors. UnCert-CoT dynamically invokes CoT reasoning based on the model's uncertainty at the point of generating a new line of code. The paper proposes two uncertainty measures: Entropy-based and Probability Differential-based methods. When uncertainty is high, CoT-decoding is activated to generate multiple reasoning paths and select the most likely correct code. When uncertainty is low, the LLM generates code directly. The authors demonstrate significant improvements in code generation accuracy on the HumanEval and MHPP benchmarks, especially on tasks requiring complex reasoning. They evaluate their approach across different LLMs and analyze the impact of various parameters.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of dynamically invoking CoT reasoning based on uncertainty is novel and relevant. Existing CoT methods often apply reasoning indiscriminately, leading to inefficiencies and potential errors. UnCert-CoT offers a more nuanced and adaptive approach that can improve both accuracy and efficiency. The use of uncertainty estimation techniques, especially in the context of code generation, is a strong point. The paper builds on and extends existing work on CoT, uncertainty estimation, and code generation, resulting in a unique contribution. However, the two proposed uncertainty metrics are relatively simple; there are potentially more sophisticated uncertainty measures that could be explored.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant problem in the application of CoT to code generation – the tendency for LLMs to "overthink." This issue can hinder the adoption of CoT methods in real-world applications, where efficiency and accuracy are paramount. The performance improvements demonstrated on the challenging MHPP dataset suggest that UnCert-CoT can be effective in improving code generation quality.  Furthermore, demonstrating consistent improvement across different LLMs strengthens the significance and applicability of their approach.  The idea of a "smart CoT," where reasoning is applied judiciously, aligns well with the broader goals of efficient and reliable LLM usage.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The core idea of uncertainty-guided reasoning is well-motivated and addresses a practical limitation of existing CoT methods.
    *   The experimental results are strong, demonstrating consistent improvements on multiple benchmarks and across different LLMs.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of each component of UnCert-CoT.
    *   The hyperparameter analysis demonstrates the robustness of the approach.
    *   The case study illustrates the benefits of UnCert-CoT in a concrete example.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The uncertainty measures used are relatively simple. Exploring more sophisticated uncertainty estimation techniques could potentially lead to further improvements.
    *   The computational overhead of calculating uncertainty at each line of code is not explicitly addressed. While the paper claims efficiency gains by avoiding unnecessary reasoning, the cost of uncertainty estimation needs to be considered.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the types of errors that UnCert-CoT is able to correct.
    *   The focus is on relatively simple Python code generation tasks. It would be valuable to evaluate the approach on more complex, real-world coding scenarios.
    * The paper would benefit from comparing to more recent techniques in structured CoT such as SCOT.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on code generation with LLMs. The concept of uncertainty-guided reasoning is applicable beyond code generation and could inspire similar approaches in other domains. The techniques used for uncertainty estimation and CoT-decoding are also valuable contributions.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to address a significant problem in code generation with LLMs. The experimental results are compelling, and the paper provides valuable insights into the benefits of uncertainty-guided reasoning. While the paper has some weaknesses, such as the simplicity of the uncertainty measures and the limited evaluation on complex coding scenarios, the overall contribution is significant and likely to influence future research in the field. The score reflects the paper's significant contribution balanced with its identified limitations.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### TruthLens:A Training-Free Paradigm for DeepFake Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15342v1)
- **Authors**: Ritabrata Chakraborty, Rajatsubhra Chakraborty, Ali Khaleghi Rahimian, Thomas MacDougall
- **Abstract**: The proliferation of synthetic images generated by advanced AI models poses significant challenges in identifying and understanding manipulated visual content. Current fake image detection methods predominantly rely on binary classification models that focus on accuracy while often neglecting interpretability, leaving users without clear insights into why an image is deemed real or fake. To bridge this gap, we introduce TruthLens, a novel training-free framework that reimagines deepfake detection as a visual question-answering (VQA) task. TruthLens utilizes state-of-the-art large vision-language models (LVLMs) to observe and describe visual artifacts and combines this with the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) like GPT-4 to analyze and aggregate evidence into informed decisions. By adopting a multimodal approach, TruthLens seamlessly integrates visual and semantic reasoning to not only classify images as real or fake but also provide interpretable explanations for its decisions. This transparency enhances trust and provides valuable insights into the artifacts that signal synthetic content. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that TruthLens outperforms conventional methods, achieving high accuracy on challenging datasets while maintaining a strong emphasis on explainability. By reframing deepfake detection as a reasoning-driven process, TruthLens establishes a new paradigm in combating synthetic media, combining cutting-edge performance with interpretability to address the growing threats of visual disinformation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TruthLens: A Training-Free Paradigm for DeepFake Detection":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TruthLens, a novel training-free framework for deepfake detection that reframes the problem as a visual question answering (VQA) task. TruthLens utilizes large vision-language models (LVLMs) to analyze images, identify visual artifacts, and provide interpretable explanations for its classification decisions. It combines visual and semantic reasoning to classify images as real or fake and offers justifications. The framework consists of four main steps: question generation, multimodal reasoning, textual aggregation, and final decision-making.  The authors demonstrate that TruthLens outperforms conventional methods on challenging datasets while maintaining a strong emphasis on explainability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of reframing deepfake detection as a VQA task using LVLMs is relatively novel. While LVLMs have been explored for related tasks, the specific approach of systematically querying images with predefined prompts to elicit explanations is a unique contribution.
*   **Significance:** Deepfake detection is a crucial problem with growing societal implications. The focus on interpretability, in addition to accuracy, is a significant step forward. The ability to provide human-readable explanations for decisions can increase user trust and aid in understanding the weaknesses of deepfake generation techniques. The training-free nature is also a strong benefit.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Explainability:**  The major strength of the paper is the emphasis on interpretability. Traditional deepfake detection methods are often black boxes, making it difficult to understand why an image is classified as fake. TruthLens provides detailed explanations, which can be valuable for understanding the types of artifacts that are being detected.
    *   **Training-Free:** Eliminating the need for task-specific training data is a major advantage, enabling quicker deployment and adaptation to new deepfake generation techniques.
    *   **Performance:** The experimental results demonstrate that TruthLens achieves competitive performance compared to existing methods, suggesting that the approach is effective.
    *   **Structured Framework:** The clear, well-defined four-step pipeline provides a solid foundation for future research and development.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LVLMs:** The performance of TruthLens is heavily reliant on the capabilities of the underlying LVLMs. If these models are biased or have limitations in their visual reasoning abilities, it could negatively impact the performance of the entire framework.  The paper doesn't deeply analyze the biases inherent in the LVLMs used and how those might affect outcomes.
    *   **Prompt Engineering:** The effectiveness of the question generation step depends on carefully crafted prompts. The paper only lists a few examples. A more comprehensive discussion of prompt engineering strategies would be beneficial. There is a risk of prompts being too narrow or not covering a sufficient range of potential artifacts.
    *   **Dataset Limitations:** While the paper uses common datasets (LDM, ProGAN), a more diverse range of deepfake datasets (e.g., those including audio/video) would provide a more comprehensive evaluation. Also, the scale of the datasets seems relatively small.
    *   **Quantitative Interpretability Evaluation:** While the paper includes qualitative analysis to assess interpretability, it lacks a robust quantitative evaluation of the quality of the explanations provided. Metrics like explanation fidelity or user satisfaction scores could be added to strengthen the evaluation.
*   **Potential Influence:** TruthLens has the potential to influence the field by shifting the focus from pure accuracy to explainable AI in deepfake detection. This could lead to more robust and trustworthy systems that are better equipped to combat the spread of misinformation.

**Justification of Score:**

While the idea and approach of TruthLens are innovative, there are some limitations to the empirical analysis and evaluation. Specifically, the reliance on pre-trained models and the relatively small scale of the datasets, combined with a lack of deeper exploration of bias and no significant quantitative evaluation of interpretability hold the contribution back from being considered a very high impact paper. The novelty of reframing the problem and the potential for explainability, however, still represent valuable advancement.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### SPILL: Domain-Adaptive Intent Clustering based on Selection and Pooling with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15351v1)
- **Authors**: I-Fan Lin, Faegheh Hasibi, Suzan Verberne
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we propose Selection and Pooling with Large Language Models (SPILL), an intuitive and domain-adaptive method for intent clustering without fine-tuning. Existing embeddings-based clustering methods rely on a few labeled examples or unsupervised fine-tuning to optimize results for each new dataset, which makes them less generalizable to multiple datasets. Our goal is to make these existing embedders more generalizable to new domain datasets without further fine-tuning. Inspired by our theoretical derivation and simulation results on the effectiveness of sampling and pooling techniques, we view the clustering task as a small-scale selection problem. A good solution to this problem is associated with better clustering performance. Accordingly, we propose a two-stage approach: First, for each utterance (referred to as the seed), we derive its embedding using an existing embedder. Then, we apply a distance metric to select a pool of candidates close to the seed. Because the embedder is not optimized for new datasets, in the second stage, we use an LLM to further select utterances from these candidates that share the same intent as the seed. Finally, we pool these selected candidates with the seed to derive a refined embedding for the seed. We found that our method generally outperforms directly using an embedder, and it achieves comparable results to other state-of-the-art studies, even those that use much larger models and require fine-tuning, showing its strength and efficiency. Our results indicate that our method enables existing embedders to be further improved without additional fine-tuning, making them more adaptable to new domain datasets. Additionally, viewing the clustering task as a small-scale selection problem gives the potential of using LLMs to customize clustering tasks according to the user's goals.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "SPILL: Domain-Adaptive Intent Clustering based on Selection and Pooling with Large Language Models" proposes a novel approach to intent clustering, called SPILL, which avoids fine-tuning existing embedding models for new domain datasets. The method leverages the capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) in a selection and pooling framework. First, an existing embedder extracts utterance embeddings, then candidate utterances close to a seed utterance are selected. An LLM then further refines this selection by identifying utterances that share the same intent as the seed.  Finally, these selected utterances are pooled with the seed to create a refined embedding used for clustering. The authors ground their method with theoretical analysis, supported by simulations. The experiments demonstrate that SPILL can improve performance compared to directly using existing embedders and achieves results comparable to state-of-the-art methods that require fine-tuning, using benchmark intent datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The main novelty lies in the specific combination of selection and pooling with LLMs, especially the explicit framing of intent clustering as a small-scale selection problem informed by a theoretical analysis concerning variance reduction. The individual components (using LLMs for intent tasks, embedding then refining) are not entirely novel, but the way they are combined and justified *is*. Also, the stated design goal of being easily implementable *without* fine-tuning while still achieving competitive performance is a worthy goal.
* **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from several factors. The efficiency of SPILL, avoiding costly fine-tuning, contributes practically to a resource-constrained environment. The domain adaptation capabilities, demonstrated through strong empirical results on benchmark datasets, represent a valuable improvement in intent clustering for real-world applications. The framework also highlights the potential of LLMs not just as end-to-end solutions, but as components within a larger system, enhancing established techniques.
* **Strengths:**
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The paper is grounded in theoretical analysis demonstrating the effectiveness of sampling and pooling, making the approach more than just a heuristic.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The method is thoroughly validated through simulations and experiments on a diverse set of benchmark datasets, showing consistent performance improvements.
    *   **Efficiency:**  SPILL does not require fine-tuning. This is a key advantage in dynamic real-world settings.
    *   **Accessibility:** The code is publicly available, promoting reproducibility and further development.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Prompt Engineering Discussion:** Although the authors aim for generality, the limited focus on prompt engineering leaves room for further optimization specific to different LLMs and datasets.
    * **Reliance on Existing Embedders:** The method's performance is bounded by the quality of the initial embedder. While it improves *existing* embedders, it's not a complete solution on its own.
    *   **Qualitative Analysis:** While there's an illustrative qualitative analysis, a deeper dive into the types of errors corrected by SPILL and the cases where it fails would be valuable.

*   **Impact:**
    *   The paper's findings could influence future research in intent clustering by promoting the use of LLMs in efficient, modular architectures, focusing on how they can augment existing methods.
    *   The ease of implementation and competitive performance could lead to the adoption of SPILL in real-world applications, such as chatbot development and customer service automation.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a well-reasoned and well-validated approach to intent clustering. The theoretical grounding and practical efficiency are key strengths.  While not a groundbreaking paradigm shift, it presents a significant and immediately useful technique. Given the clear contributions, the strong empirical validation, and the practical significance of avoiding fine-tuning, I assign a score of:

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SemEval-2025 Task 1: AdMIRe -- Advancing Multimodal Idiomaticity Representation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15358v1)
- **Authors**: Thomas Pickard, Aline Villavicencio, Maggie Mi, Wei He, Dylan Phelps, Carolina Scarton, Marco Idiart
- **Abstract**: Idiomatic expressions present a unique challenge in NLP, as their meanings are often not directly inferable from their constituent words. Despite recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs), idiomaticity remains a significant obstacle to robust semantic representation. We present datasets and tasks for SemEval-2025 Task 1: AdMiRe (Advancing Multimodal Idiomaticity Representation), which challenges the community to assess and improve models' ability to interpret idiomatic expressions in multimodal contexts and in multiple languages. Participants competed in two subtasks: ranking images based on their alignment with idiomatic or literal meanings, and predicting the next image in a sequence. The most effective methods achieved human-level performance by leveraging pretrained LLMs and vision-language models in mixture-of-experts settings, with multiple queries used to smooth over the weaknesses in these models' representations of idiomaticity.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the SemEval-2025 Task 1 paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces the AdMIRe (Advancing Multimodal Idiomaticity Representation) task for SemEval-2025. The task focuses on evaluating models' understanding of idiomatic expressions in multimodal contexts (text and images) and across multiple languages (English and Brazilian Portuguese).  The paper details the construction of two subtasks:
*   **Subtask A (Multiple Image Choice):**  Ranking images or image captions based on their alignment with the literal or idiomatic meaning of a target expression.
*   **Subtask B (Image Sequences/Next Image Prediction):**  Completing image sequences that depict either the literal or idiomatic meaning of an expression, along with identifying whether the depicted sense is literal or idiomatic.

The paper also summarizes the approaches taken by participating teams (primarily leveraging LLMs and vision-language models), highlighting key findings, challenges encountered (e.g., LLMs' bias toward idiomatic interpretations), and a human evaluation of the task.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Dataset:** The creation of a new multimodal dataset for idiomatic expressions is a valuable contribution. Existing datasets tend to focus solely on text or lack a controlled exploration of both literal and figurative senses within a visual context. Including Brazilian Portuguese is also a positive, albeit not groundbreaking, addition.  The effort to construct image sequences representing temporal aspects of idiomatic meaning is a distinct feature.
*   **Task Design:**  The ranking and next-image-prediction tasks are reasonably well-defined and aligned with the goal of assessing models' understanding of idiomatic expressions. The inclusion of "distractor" images and alternatives to the final sequence image adds complexity and forces models to consider subtle semantic differences.
*   **Analysis of Results:**  The paper offers a good overview of the participating systems and their performance.  The discussion of LLM biases and the effectiveness of specific techniques (e.g., mixtures of experts, data augmentation) is insightful.

**Significance:**

*   **Addressing a Core NLP Challenge:** Idiomaticity remains a significant hurdle for NLP models.  This task directly targets that challenge, pushing the field to develop more robust semantic representations.
*   **Multimodal Approach:**  The multimodal focus is crucial. It recognizes that visual information can play a key role in understanding and disambiguating idiomatic expressions.
*   **Benchmarking LLMs:**  The task provides a valuable benchmark for evaluating the performance of LLMs and vision-language models on a complex semantic task, revealing their strengths and weaknesses. The insight that LLMs are biased towards the idiomatic interpretation is also of significance.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset Size:** The dataset size is a notable limitation. While carefully curated, a larger dataset would have allowed for more robust training and evaluation.  The limited number of submissions for Subtask B (Image Sequences) further reduces the significance of the findings in that area.
*   **Image Generation Concerns:** While the use of Midjourney allowed for controlled image generation, the reliance on a specific model and prompts inevitably introduces biases.  The paper acknowledges this but perhaps doesn't fully explore the potential impact on the task. Furthermore, the style and quality of the generated images are inconsistent and could introduce noise.
*   **Evaluation Metrics:** While DCG is a standard metric, the somewhat arbitrary weighting of the image rankings in Subtask A raises questions about its sensitivity and interpretability. Top-1 accuracy can be an oversimplified measure in this context.
*   **Scope:** The focus is fairly narrow, concentrating primarily on nominal compounds. While justifiable to limit scope, the results may not generalize well to other types of idiomatic expressions.
*   **Reproducibility:**  The paper lacks sufficient detail to reproduce the entire process. Specific prompts, hyperparameter settings for training models, and the data generation method are not discussed enough to enable someone to duplicate the work.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the above analysis, a score of **7** seems appropriate.

*   **The positive aspects** include the creation of a novel multimodal dataset, a well-defined task design, and valuable insights into the performance of LLMs and vision-language models on idiomaticity. The inclusion of two subtasks that evaluate different aspects of image understanding makes the task richer.
*   **However, the limitations** regarding dataset size, potential biases in image generation, the choice of some of the evaluation metrics, the scope of the dataset, and reproducibility hold the paper back from a higher score.

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field, but there is room for improvement in future iterations to address the noted limitations. This is a timely contribution that provides new insights to the area.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 0/10

### EfficientLLaVA:Generalizable Auto-Pruning for Large Vision-language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15369v1)
- **Authors**: Yinan Liang, Ziwei Wang, Xiuwei Xu, Jie Zhou, Jiwen Lu
- **Abstract**: While multimodal large language models demonstrate strong performance in complex reasoning tasks, they pose significant challenges related to model complexity during deployment, especially for resource-limited devices. In this paper, we propose an automatic pruning method for large vision-language models to enhance the efficiency of multimodal reasoning. Conventional methods rely on the training data of the original model to select the proper pruning ratio for different network components. However, these methods are impractical for large vision-language models due to the unaffordable search costs caused by web-scale training corpus. In contrast, our approach only leverages a small number of samples to search for the desired pruning policy by maximizing its generalization ability on unknown training data while maintaining the model accuracy, which enables the achievement of an optimal trade-off between accuracy and efficiency for large visual language models. Specifically, we formulate the generalization gap of the pruning strategy using the structural risk minimization principle. Based on both task performance and generalization capability, we iteratively search for the optimal pruning policy within a given search space and optimize the vision projector to evolve the search space with higher upper bound of performance. We conduct extensive experiments on the ScienceQA, Vizwiz, MM-vet, and LLaVA-Bench datasets for the task of visual question answering. Using only 64 samples for pruning policy search, EfficientLLaVA achieves an accuracy of 83.05% on ScienceQA, along with a $\times$ 1.8 speedup compared to the dense LLaVA-v1.5-7B model.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "EfficientLLaVA: Generalizable Auto-Pruning for Large Vision-language Models" introduces a novel automatic pruning method for Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs). The core idea is to improve efficiency (reduce model complexity and inference latency) without significantly sacrificing task performance, especially when deploying on resource-constrained devices.  Instead of relying on large-scale training data of the original model (which is computationally expensive), the proposed approach uses a small number of proxy samples to search for the optimal pruning policy.  The method maximizes the *generalization ability* of the pruning policy to unknown training data by leveraging the structural risk minimization (SRM) principle. The approach iteratively searches for the best pruning policy within a defined space and optimizes the vision projector to enhance accuracy and generalizability. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of EfficientLLaVA on several VQA datasets (ScienceQA, Vizwiz, MM-vet, and LLaVA-Bench), achieving a good balance between accuracy and speedup.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates some novelty in its approach to pruning LVLMs. The key novel aspect is the use of structural risk minimization (SRM) to maximize the *generalization ability* of the pruning policy *with a small amount of proxy data*. Conventional pruning methods rely on the original, large-scale training data. The idea of evolving the search space via vision projector optimization is also a contribution, albeit an incremental one. The explicit focus on minimizing the gap between performance on proxy samples and unseen data, within the context of large LVLM pruning, appears to be a fresh perspective.

*   **Significance:** Pruning LVLMs for deployment on resource-constrained devices is a highly relevant and significant problem.  The demonstrated speedups (e.g., 1.8x on ScienceQA) while maintaining competitive accuracy have practical implications. The paper addresses a key barrier to the wider adoption of these models. The use of the method across a set of question-answer datasets also demonstrates some generalizability of the solution, that can be applied on other problems beyond the specific datasets evaluated.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Generalization focus:** The emphasis on improving the generalization of the pruning policy, rather than just optimizing for the proxy samples, is a crucial aspect. This differentiates the paper from many traditional pruning methods.
    *   **Low data requirement:** The ability to achieve competitive results with only a small amount of data is a significant practical advantage, as it makes the method applicable to scenarios where the original training data is unavailable or prohibitively expensive.
    *   **Experimental validation:** The paper includes experiments on several established datasets.
    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper addresses a well-defined and important problem.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental novelty:** While the combination of SRM and search space evolution is novel, the individual components are not entirely new. The pruning algorithms (OBS, SparseGPT) are existing methods.
    *   **Limited theoretical depth:** The paper presents the SRM formulation, but a deeper theoretical analysis of the generalization bound would strengthen the contribution. A more thorough discussion of the assumptions underlying the SRM approach, and its limitations in the context of LVLMs, is warranted.
    *   **Limited hardware evaluation:** While the paper reports speedup numbers, further practical evaluation on actual edge devices (e.g., mobile phones, embedded systems) would enhance the significance of the work. The CPU-based acceleration is promising, but more comprehensive benchmarking with optimized kernels (CUTLASS on NVIDIA GPUs) and on edge devices would be valuable.
    *   **Clarity and presentation:**  There's room for improving the clarity of certain sections, particularly the descriptions of the search space evolution and the weighted fitness function. The writing could be more accessible.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence research in the direction of more data-efficient and generalizable pruning methods for LVLMs. If the method proves robust across a wider range of models, tasks, and hardware platforms, it could become a valuable tool for deploying these models in real-world applications.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

The paper addresses a significant problem in a relatively novel way, showing good experimental results and a focus on generalization. However, the theoretical grounding could be improved, and a more extensive hardware evaluation is needed. While the individual components are not entirely new, the combination and the focus on SRM for LVLMs using limited proxy data makes the approach worthwhile. Therefore a score of 7 is justifiable.

### CCDP: Composition of Conditional Diffusion Policies with Guided Sampling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15386v1)
- **Authors**: Amirreza Razmjoo, Sylvain Calinon, Michael Gienger, Fan Zhang
- **Abstract**: Imitation Learning offers a promising approach to learn directly from data without requiring explicit models, simulations, or detailed task definitions. During inference, actions are sampled from the learned distribution and executed on the robot. However, sampled actions may fail for various reasons, and simply repeating the sampling step until a successful action is obtained can be inefficient. In this work, we propose an enhanced sampling strategy that refines the sampling distribution to avoid previously unsuccessful actions. We demonstrate that by solely utilizing data from successful demonstrations, our method can infer recovery actions without the need for additional exploratory behavior or a high-level controller. Furthermore, we leverage the concept of diffusion model decomposition to break down the primary problem (which may require long-horizon history to manage failures) into multiple smaller, more manageable sub-problems in learning, data collection, and inference, thereby enabling the system to adapt to variable failure counts. Our approach yields a low-level controller that dynamically adjusts its sampling space to improve efficiency when prior samples fall short. We validate our method across several tasks, including door opening with unknown directions, object manipulation, and button-searching scenarios, demonstrating that our approach outperforms traditional baselines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "CCDP: Composition of Conditional Diffusion Policies with Guided Sampling":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of robots recovering from failed actions when learning from demonstration data that only contains successful examples. The core idea is to enhance a diffusion policy by incorporating a mechanism to guide the action sampling away from previously unsuccessful actions. The method, called Composition of Conditional Diffusion Policies (CCDP), decomposes the overall action distribution into a composition of smaller, more manageable sub-problems.  This decomposition includes terms to encourage actions similar to demonstrations, actions matching the current state, actions consistent with the history, and, crucially, actions that avoid regions in the action space associated with past failures. The paper synthesizes a failure dataset by defining recovery actions as those dissimilar to the failed action in a key feature space. CCDP then uses this information to guide action sampling during inference.  The method is validated on various tasks, including door opening with unknown directions, object manipulation, button searching, object packing and bartending. Experimental results show improvements in success rates and implicit objective fulfillment compared to a standard diffusion policy and a rejection sampling-based enhanced diffusion policy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of *actively* steering the action space away from previously failed actions within a diffusion policy framework is a valuable contribution.  It distinguishes itself from traditional imitation learning, which often focuses on replicating successful actions or on techniques that need external models for recovery behaviors. The decomposition of the diffusion policy into state, history, and failure components is a reasonable and provides modularity. Specifically, the usage of the key feature is an interesting approach to create a flexible control that is easy to maintain.

*   **Significance:**  The paper tackles a significant practical problem: how robots can recover from failures when learning from demonstration, a common scenario where negative examples are rare or unavailable. The approach of synthesizing failure data is particularly useful as it removes the dependency on a realistic simulator, which can be a bottleneck. The experimental results, while conducted in simulation, demonstrate a clear improvement over the baselines, suggesting the potential for real-world applications. The extension of the work to bartending, also showcases the ability to go beyond basic failure and apply the key features to implicit objectives to control the robot behavior.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a highly relevant problem in robotics.
    *   The CCDP approach offers a novel and effective way to guide action sampling away from failure regions.
    *   The modular decomposition of the diffusion policy is well-motivated and provides flexibility.
    *   Clear experimental validation with comprehensive comparisons to baselines.
    *   Presents a practically viable method as it does not rely on simulations during training or exploration.
    *   The assumption on the static cause of failures is reasonable for many common robotic tasks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method assumes that the cause of the failure remains static.
    *   The failure key features are currently defined manually, which limits the adaptability and scalability of the approach.
    *   The definition of dissimilar actions as valid recoveries may not always hold and could be suboptimal in some scenarios.
    *   The experiments are conducted in simulation, and while the results are promising, it's uncertain whether the system can withstand noises in the real world.
    *   The improvement on the OM tasks is not as big as the other task.
    *   The weighting of the different terms in the decomposed diffusion policy is not fully explored.
    *   While the assumption on the static cause of the failure is reasonable, it limits the method in dynamic scenarios.
    *   The reliance on pre-defined motion planning for data collecting may result in an over fitting behavior.
    *   The use of pre-defined motion planning and expert access in simulated environments could limit generalizability of the learned behaviors to real-world scenarios, and also the robustness.

*   **Potential Influence:** The CCDP method could influence research on imitation learning, particularly in scenarios where failure recovery is crucial but negative data is scarce. It could inspire the development of more sophisticated and adaptable failure key feature extraction techniques, as well as methods for dynamically adjusting the combination weights in the decomposed diffusion policy. This would improve generalization and scalability of robot learning in more complicated scenarios.

*   **Justification for Score:**
    The paper presents a novel and practically relevant approach to failure recovery in imitation learning. The CCDP method and its decomposition of the diffusion policy are well-motivated, and the experimental results provide compelling evidence of its effectiveness. Although it suffers from the fact that simulations are not as useful with noises, they do illustrate the usefulness of the approach. The method also requires manual definition of the key features and the static of failure. Despite these limitations, the paper represents a significant contribution to the field. The work tackles an important problem in imitation learning and provides a good novel solution without requiring extensive computational resources or external information. The paper presents all aspects clearly and in a well organized fashion. The limitations are thoroughly addressed and potential improvement discussed clearly.
